Lattice Models, Differential Forms, and the Yang-Baxter Equation
Kedar Karhadkar

Abstract
We introduce new methods to describe admissible states of the six-vertex and the eight-vertex lattice models of statistical mechanics. For the six-vertex model, we view the admissible states as differential forms on a grid graph. This yields a new proof of the correspondence between admissible states and 3-colorings of a rectangular grid. For the eight-vertex model, we interpret the set of admissible states as an 𝔽 2 \mathbb{F}_{2} -vector space. This viewpoint lets us enumerate the set of admissible states. Finally, we find necessary conditions for a Yang-Baxter equation to hold for the general eight-vertex model.



1
Introduction

Lattice models are objects from statistical mechanics which have also seen a number of surprising applications in mathematics. These models are built with a graph (often a rectangular grid) whose edges are labeled, and locally satisfy some property. If this property is satisfied, we call the state
admissible
. Each vertex of the graph is assigned a weight depending on the labels of the edges around it. The goal is to make conclusions about the global behavior of the graph. One way of studying this is by determining the
partition function
of the lattice model, which is calculated by taking a product of the vertex weights, and summing over all possible states:

Z(\mathfrak{G})=\sum_{\text{states}}\prod_{v\in V}\text{wt}(v).

Baxter
[
1
]
studied various models in this way. An important method that he used was repeated application of the
Yang-Baxter equation
, from which he deduced certain symmetry properties of partition functions. These symmetries are the source of several connections to representation theory. For instance, if we assign the vertices a particular set of weights, then the partition function we obtain is a Schur function
[
2
]
. This allows one to prove symmetric function identities using lattice models, such as the dual Cauchy identity
[
4
]
and the Weyl character formula
[
3
]
.

We will primarily focus our attention on the six-vertex and the eight-vertex square lattice models. The six-vertex model was famously used by Kuperberg
[
9
]
to prove the alternating sign matrix conjecture, originally proven by Zeilberger
[
11
]
. Among the first steps of the proof was a correspondence between admissible lattice states of the six vertex model and alternating sign matrices. In this paper, we will find combinatorial interpretations of lattice states of a similar flavor.

The primary technique that we will use to study the states of lattice models will be the use of discrete differential forms on a rectangular grid. Discrete differential calculus has been studied recently over arbitrary graphs; for a more general description of it, see
[
8
]
. Our methods rely on the observation that for certain edge values
f_{i,j}
and
g_{i,j}
, we have

g_{i+1,j}-g_{i,j}=f_{i,j+1}-f_{i,j}.

We rewrite this as

D_{x}g=D_{y}f,

where
D_{x}
and
D_{y}
are
discrete partial derivatives
. Drawing an analogy to the continuous case, we say that the 1-form
fdx+gdy
is
closed
. Since rectangular grids are discrete analogues of open balls in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, we see that closed 1-forms on the rectangular grid are exact. This gives us a new proof that there are three times as many 3-colorings of a rectangular grid as there are admissible lattice states in the six-vertex model, a fact proven by Lenard
[
10
]
.

Our viewpoint becomes more fruitful when we look at more complicated lattices. Specifically, we study the six-vertex model with toroidal boundary conditions. This is a discrete analogue of a torus, so we expect it to have nontrivial cohomology. By computing this cohomology, we are able to find a new combinatorial interpretation of the admissible states with toroidal boundary conditions. In particular, we see that the number of admissible states are not simply in correspondence with 3-colorings of a toroidal grid graph.

After studying the six-vertex model, we turn our attention to admissible states of the eight-vertex model. Although we could use our previous differential forms strategy, we find that the structure of the admissible states is even simpler. In particular, there is a natural description of the admissible states as a vector space over
\mathbb{F}_{2}
. This lets us explicitly determine the number of admissible states in the eight-vertex model. Moreover, we can determine this number for any given set of boundary conditions, and it is essentially independent of the boundary.

Finally, we turn our attention back to the Yang-Baxter equation in the eight-vertex model. Partition functions for the eight-vertex model have been studied by Fan and Wu
[
6
]
and Galleas and Martins
[
7
]
. In their analysis, they make the assumption that for certain parameters
c_{1},c_{-1},d_{1},d_{-1}
, we have
c_{1}=c_{-1}
and
d_{1}=d_{-1}
. We prove our results while relaxing these assumptions, so we are able to generalize their previous work. We determine conditions the Boltzmann weights of the lattice that are required in order to find a solution to the Yang-Baxter equation.

2
Admissible States of the Six-Vertex Model

Here we will quickly define the six-vertex lattice model. Let us consider a rectangular grid in which the edges are labeled as either 0 or 1. We will refer to such a labeling of the edges as a
state
.

Figure 2:
Admissible labelings in the six-vertex model.

If all of the vertices of a state are in one of the six configurations of Figure 2, then we call the state
admissible
. For instance, the state in Figure 1 is admissible.

For the rest of this section, as well as sections 3 and 4, fix
m,n\geq 2
. For a rectangular lattice with
m
columns and
n
rows of interior vertices, let
v_{i,j}
denote the vertex that is in the
i
-th column from the left and the
j
-th row from the bottom. Let
g_{i,j}
be the label of the horizontal edge which is
i
-th from the left and
j
-th from the bottom. Similarly, let
f_{i,j}
be the label of the vertical edge which is
i
-th from the left and
j
-th from the bottom. For
1\leq i\leq m
and
1\leq j\leq n
, we call the entries
g_{1,j},g_{m+1,j},f_{i,1},f_{i,n+1}
the
boundary values
of the lattice. At any vertex
v_{i,j}
, we have the following labeling.

g_{i,j}
f_{i,j+1}
g_{i+1,j}
f_{i,j}
\bullet

It is easy to verify that this labeling is admissible if and only if

\displaystyle g_{i+1,j}-g_{i,j}\equiv f_{i,j+1}-f_{i,j}\pmod{3}.

(1)

In light of this, we will view
f
and
g
as functions from
[m]\times[n+1]
to
\mathbb{F}_{3}
and
[m+1]\times[n]
to
\mathbb{F}_{3}
respectively, where
[n]
denotes the set
\{1,2,\cdots,n\}
.

Let
h:[m+1]\times[n+1]\to\mathbb{F}_{3}
be a function. We define its
discrete partial derivatives

D_{x}h:[m]\times[n+1]\to\mathbb{F}_{3}
and
D_{y}h:[m+1]\times[n]\to\mathbb{F}_{3}
by

(D_{x}h)_{i,j}=h_{i+1,j}-h_{i,j}

and

(D_{y}h)_{i,j}=h_{i,j+1}-h_{i,j}.

We define a (discrete)
1-form
to be a formal expression

fdx+gdy,

where
f:[m]\times[n+1]\to\mathbb{F}_{3}
and
g:[m+1]\times[n]\to\mathbb{F}_{3}
are functions. If
h:[m+1]\times[n+1]\to\mathbb{F}_{3}
is a function, we define its
exterior derivative

dh
by

dh=(D_{x}h)dx+(D_{y}h)dy.

We say that a 1-form
fdx+gdy
is
closed
if

D_{y}f=D_{x}g,

and
exact
if
fdx+gdy=dh
for some function
h
. It is easy to see that every exact 1-form is closed. In fact, by the following discrete version of the Poincaré lemma, the converse is also true.

Lemma 1
.

Let
\alpha=fdx+gdy
be a closed 1-form. Then
\alpha
is exact.

Proof.

Define
h:[m+1]\times[n+1]\to\mathbb{F}_{3}
by

h_{i,j}=\sum_{a=1}^{i-1}f_{a,1}+\sum_{b=1}^{j-1}g_{i,b}.

Then,

\displaystyle(D_{y}h)_{i,j}

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{a=1}^{i-1}f_{a,1}+\sum_{b=1}^{j}g_{i,b}\right)-\left(\sum_{a=1}^{i}f_{a,1}+\sum_{b=1}^{j-1}g_{i,b}\right)

\displaystyle=g_{i,j}

and

\displaystyle(D_{x}h)_{i,j}

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{a=1}^{i}f_{a,1}+\sum_{b=1}^{j-1}g_{i+1,b}\right)-\left(\sum_{a=1}^{i-1}f_{a,1}+\sum_{b=1}^{j-1}g_{i,b}\right)

\displaystyle=f_{i,1}+\sum_{b=1}^{j-1}(D_{x}g)_{i,b}

\displaystyle=f_{i,1}+\sum_{b=1}^{j-1}(D_{y}f)_{i,b}

\displaystyle=f_{i,j}.

So
dh=\alpha
, and
\alpha
is exact.
∎

Call a 1-form
fdx+gdy

admissible
if for all
i
and
j
,
f_{i,j}
and
g_{i,j}
are not equal to 2. Using this language, we can describe admissible states in terms of differential forms.

Lemma 2
.

There is a one-to-one correspondence between admissible states of the six-vertex model and admissible closed 1-forms.

Proof.

Consider an admissible state
A
of a lattice with with
m
columns and
n
rows. Let
g_{i,j}
and
f_{i,j}
be the entries of the horizontal and vertical edges as above. Then for all
i
and
j
,
g_{i,j}
and
f_{i,j}
are not equal to 2. Equation (
1
) holds exactly when

D_{y}f=D_{x}g,

so we associate
A
with the admissible closed 1-form
fdx+gdy
.
∎

As an application of the above correspondence, we will describe admissible states in terms of colorings of a rectangular grid. For the original proof of this result, see
[
10
]
. Recall that a
k
-coloring of a graph is an assignment of values from the set
\{0,1,\cdots,k-1\}
to each vertex of the graph, such that two adjacent vertices are not assigned the same value.

Theorem 3
.

There are three times as many 3-colorings of a rectangular grid with
m+1
columns and
n+1
rows as there are admissible states of the six-vertex model.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{S}_{1}
denote the set of admissible closed 1-forms, and let
\mathcal{S}_{2}
denote the set of functions
h:[m+1]\times[n+1]\to\mathbb{F}_{3}
such that for all
i
and
j
,
h_{i+1,j}\neq h_{i,j}
and
h_{i,j+1}\neq h_{i,j}
. In other words,
\mathcal{S}_{2}
is the set of functions
h
such that
D_{x}h
and
D_{y}h
are nonzero everywhere. It is easy to see that
\mathcal{S}_{2}
is in bijection with the set of 3-colorings of the rectangular grid. To prove the theorem, then, it suffices to find a bijection between
\mathcal{S}_{1}\times\mathbb{F}_{3}
and
\mathcal{S}_{2}
. By Lemma
1
, every element of
\mathcal{S}_{1}
is exact, so we can write every element of
\mathcal{S}_{1}
in the form
dh
, where
h:[m+1]\times[n+1]\to\mathbb{F}_{3}
is a function. Now define
F:\mathcal{S}_{1}\times\mathbb{F}_{3}\to\mathcal{S}_{2}
by

F(dh,t)_{i,j}=h_{i,j}-h_{1,1}+t+i+j-2.

Note that
F
is well-defined, since if
dh=dh^{\prime}
, then
h-h^{\prime}
is constant, and
h_{i,j}-h_{1,1}=h^{\prime}_{i,j}-h^{\prime}_{1,1}
for all
i,j
. To see that
F
does indeed map
\mathcal{S}_{1}\times\mathbb{F}_{3}
into
\mathcal{S}_{2}
, observe that

\displaystyle(D_{x}F(dh,t))dx+(D_{y}F(dh,t))dy

\displaystyle=dF(dh,t)

\displaystyle=dh+dx+dy.

If we write
dh=fdx+gdy
, then
f
and
g
are nowhere equal to 2, since
dh
is admissible. So, comparing the first and last expressions in the equation above, we see that
D_{x}F(dh,t)
and
D_{y}F(dh,t)
are nowhere 0. Thus,
F
is a well-defined map into
\mathcal{S}_{2}
. Next, we define
G:\mathcal{S}_{2}\to\mathcal{S}_{1}\times\mathbb{F}_{3}
by

G(h)=(dh-dx-dy,h_{1,1}).

To see that
G
maps
\mathcal{S}_{2}
into
\mathcal{S}_{1}\times\mathbb{F}_{3}
, observe that if we write
dh=fdx+gdy
, then
f
and
g
are nowhere zero, so the components of
dh-dx-dy
are nowhere equal to 2. Now let
x,y:[m+1]\times[n+1]\to\mathbb{F}_{3}
be functions defined by

\displaystyle x_{i,j}

\displaystyle=i

\displaystyle y_{i,j}

\displaystyle=j.

Then we see that

\displaystyle(F\circ G)(h)_{i,j}

\displaystyle=F(d(h-x-y),h_{1,1})_{i,j}

\displaystyle=(h_{i,j}-x_{i,j}-y_{i,j})-(h_{1,1}-x_{1,1}-y_{1,1})+h_{1,1}+i+j-2

\displaystyle=h_{i,j}

and

\displaystyle(G\circ F)(dh,t)

\displaystyle=(dF(dh,t)-dx-dy,F(dh,t)_{1,1})

\displaystyle=((dh+dx+dy)-dx-dy,t)

\displaystyle=(dh,t).

Thus,
F
and
G
are inverses of each other, and
\mathcal{S}_{1}\times\mathbb{F}_{3}
and
\mathcal{S}_{2}
are in bijection.
∎

3
Toroidal Boundary Conditions

We will now apply the methods used in the previous section to lattices with toroidal boundary conditions. In this section, we will make the additional assumption that
m
and
n
are not divisible by 3. Let
A
be an admissible state of the six-vertex model, with vertical entries
f_{i,j}
and horizontal entries
g_{i,j}
as previously. We say that
A
has
toroidal boundary conditions
if its boundary values satisfy

\displaystyle g_{1,j}

\displaystyle=g_{m+1,j}

\displaystyle f_{i,1}

\displaystyle=f_{i,n+1}

for all
1\leq i\leq m
and
1\leq j\leq n
.

\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet

Figure 3:
An admissible state with toroidal boundary conditions.

If
h:\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{F}_{3}
is a function, we say that
h
is
doubly periodic
if

\displaystyle h_{i+m,j}=h_{i,j+n}=h_{i,j}

for all
i,j\in\mathbb{Z}
. Just as before, for such a function
h
, we define its
partial derivatives

D_{x}h,D_{y}h:\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{F}_{3}
by

(D_{x}h)_{i,j}=h_{i+1,j}-h_{i,j}

and

(D_{y}h)_{i,j}=h_{i,j+1}-h_{i,j}.

Note that
D_{x}h
and
D_{y}h
are doubly periodic. We define a
toroidal 1-form
to be a formal expression
fdx+gdy
, where
f,g:\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{F}_{3}
are doubly periodic functions. For a doubly periodic function
h
, we define its
exterior derivative

dh
as the toroidal 1-form given by

dh=(D_{x}h)dx+(D_{y}h)dy.

We say that a toroidal 1-form
fdx+gdy
is
closed
if

D_{y}f=D_{x}g

and
exact
if
fdx+gdy=dh
for some doubly periodic
h:\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{F}_{3}
. A toroidal 1-form
fdx+gdy
is
admissible
if for all
i
and
j
,
f_{i,j}
and
g_{i,j}
are not equal to 2. In this section, we will simply refer to a toroidal 1-form as a 1-form. The following lemma is an analogue of Lemma
1
; it computes the 1-dimensional cohomology of the discrete torus.

Lemma 4
.

Every closed 1-form can be written uniquely in the form

rdx+sdy+\omega,

where
r,s\in\mathbb{F}_{3}
and
\omega
is exact.

Proof.

Let
fdx+gdy
be a closed 1-form, where
f,g:\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{F}_{3}
are doubly periodic. Let

\displaystyle r

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}f_{i,1},

\displaystyle s

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}g_{1,j}.

We claim that
\omega=fdx+gdy-rdx-sdy
is exact. To see this, define a function
h:\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{F}_{3}
by

h_{i,j}=\sum_{a=1}^{\tilde{i}-1}(f_{a,1}-r)+\sum_{b=1}^{\tilde{j}-1}(g_{i,b}-s),

where
\tilde{i}
is the unique integer such that
1\leq\tilde{i}\leq m
with
\tilde{i}\equiv i\pmod{m}
, and
\tilde{j}
is the integer such that
1\leq\tilde{j}\leq n
with
\tilde{j}\equiv j\pmod{n}
. It is clear that
h
is doubly periodic. Observe that if
i\not\equiv 0\pmod{m}
, we have
\widetilde{i+1}=\tilde{i}+1
, so

\sum_{a=1}^{\widetilde{i+1}-1}(f_{a,1}-r)=\sum_{a=1}^{\tilde{i}}(f_{a,1}-r).

If
i\equiv 0\pmod{m}
, then we also have

\displaystyle\sum_{a=1}^{\widetilde{i+1}-1}(f_{a,1}-r)

\displaystyle=\sum_{a=1}^{0}(f_{a,1}-r)

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle=\sum_{a=1}^{m}(f_{a,1}-r)

\displaystyle=\sum_{a=1}^{\tilde{i}}(f_{a,1}-r).

A similar argument shows that for all
j
,

\sum_{b=1}^{\widetilde{j+1}-1}(g_{i,b}-s)=\sum_{b=1}^{\tilde{j}}(g_{i,b}-s).

We now compute

\displaystyle(D_{x}h)_{i,j}

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{a=1}^{\widetilde{i+1}-1}(f_{a,1}-r)+\sum_{b=1}^{\tilde{j}-1}(g_{i+1,b}-s)\right)-\left(\sum_{a=1}^{\tilde{i}-1}(f_{a,1}-r)+\sum_{b=1}^{\tilde{j}-1}(g_{i,b}-s)\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{a=1}^{\tilde{i}}(f_{a,1}-r)+\sum_{b=1}^{\tilde{j}-1}(g_{i+1,b}-s)\right)-\left(\sum_{a=1}^{\tilde{i}-1}(f_{a,1}-r)+\sum_{b=1}^{\tilde{j}-1}(g_{i,b}-s)\right)

\displaystyle=(f_{\tilde{i},1}-r)+\sum_{b=1}^{\tilde{j}-1}(g_{i+1,b}-g_{i,b})

\displaystyle=(f_{i,1}-r)+\sum_{b=1}^{\tilde{j}-1}(D_{x}g)_{i,b}

\displaystyle=(f_{i,1}-r)+\sum_{b=1}^{\tilde{j}-1}(D_{y}f)_{i,b}

\displaystyle=f_{i,\tilde{j}}-r

\displaystyle=f_{i,j}-r

and

\displaystyle(D_{y}h)_{i,j}

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{a=1}^{\tilde{i}-1}(f_{a,1}-r)+\sum_{b=1}^{\widetilde{j+1}-1}(g_{i,b}-s)\right)-\left(\sum_{a=1}^{\tilde{i}-1}(f_{a,1}-r)+\sum_{b=1}^{\tilde{j}-1}(g_{i,b}-s)\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{a=1}^{\tilde{i}-1}(f_{a,1}-r)+\sum_{b=1}^{\tilde{j}}(g_{i,b}-s)\right)-\left(\sum_{a=1}^{\tilde{i}-1}(f_{a,1}-r)+\sum_{b=1}^{\tilde{j}-1}(g_{i,b}-s)\right)

\displaystyle=g_{i,\tilde{j}}-s

\displaystyle=g_{i,j}-s.

Thus, we have shown that
dh=\omega
, so
\omega
is exact.

To check uniqueness, it suffices to show that if
rdx+sdy
is exact, then
r=s=0
. Suppose that
rdx+sdy=df
, where
f
is doubly periodic. Then

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}(f_{i+1,1}-f_{i,1})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}(D_{x}f)_{i,1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}r

\displaystyle=r.

A similar argument shows that
s=0
.
∎

Lemma 5
.

There is a one-to-one correspondence between admissible closed 1-forms and admissible states of the six-vertex model with toroidal boundary conditions.

Proof.

Let
f_{i,j}
and
g_{i,j}
be the vertical and horizontal entries of an admissible state
A
with toroidal boundary conditions. Then we may uniquely extend
f_{i,j}
and
g_{i,j}
to doubly periodic functions
\tilde{f},\tilde{g}:\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{F}_{3}
. We associate
A
with the closed admissible 1-form
\tilde{f}dx+\tilde{g}dy
. It is not difficult to see that this is a one-to-one correspondence.
∎

Call a doubly periodic function
h:\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{F}_{3}

sparse
if neither
D_{x}h
nor
D_{y}h
is surjective, and
h_{1,1}=0
. We can characterize admissible states with toroidal boundary conditions in terms of sparse functions, in the same way that we classified admissible states in the last section in terms of 3-colorings.

Theorem 6
.

There is a one-to-one correspondence between sparse functions and admissible states of the six-vertex model with toroidal boundary conditions.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{S}_{1}
denote the set of closed admissible 1-forms, and let
\mathcal{S}_{2}
be the set of sparse functions. It suffices to find a bijection between
\mathcal{S}_{1}
and
\mathcal{S}_{2}
. Define a map
F:\mathcal{S}_{2}\to\mathcal{S}_{1}
as follows. If
h
is a sparse function, define

F(h)=r(h)dx+s(h)dy+(D_{x}h)dx+(D_{y}h)dy=r(h)dx+s(h)dy+dh,

where
2-r(h)\notin\text{Im}(D_{x}h)
and
2-s(h)\notin\text{Im}(D_{y}h)
. We will show that
F
is a bijection. To see that
F
is injective, suppose that
F(h)=F(h^{\prime})
. Then, by the uniqueness result in Lemma
4
,
dh=dh^{\prime}
, which implies that
h-h^{\prime}
is constant. But
h_{1,1}=(h^{\prime})_{1,1}=0
, so
h=h^{\prime}
and
F
is injective. To see that
F
is surjective, let

\omega=rdx+sdy+(D_{x}h)dx+(D_{y}h)dy

be an element of
\mathcal{S}_{1}
(by Lemma
4
). Without loss of generality, we may assume that
h_{1,1}=0
. Then, by definition,
r+D_{x}h
and
s+D_{y}h
are not surjective, implying that
D_{x}h
and
D_{y}h
are not surjective. So
\omega=F(h)
. Thus, we have a bijection.
∎

4
Admissible States of the Eight-Vertex Model

In this section, we will consider a different set of admissible labelings.

{{}}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.5pt}{-24.56178pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 7.87 0 C 7.87 4.35 4.35 7.87 0 7.87 C -4.35 7.87 -7.87 4.35 -7.87 0 C -7.87 -4.35 -4.35 -7.87 0 -7.87 C 4.35 -7.87 7.87 -4.35 7.87 0 Z M 0 0}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.5pt}{-2.22221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

Figure 4:
Admissible labelings in the eight-vertex model.

In the eight-vertex model, we call a state
admissible
if all of its vertices are in one of the eight configurations of Figure 4. Let
f_{i,j}
and
g_{i,j}
be the labelings of the vertical edges and horizontal edges as before. At a vertex
v_{i,j}
, we have the labeling

g_{i,j}
f_{i,j+1}
g_{i+1,j}
f_{i,j}
\bullet

as before, so a state is admissible exactly when

\displaystyle f_{i,j}+g_{i,j}+f_{i,j+1}+g_{i+1,j}\equiv 0\pmod{2}

(2)

for all
1\leq i\leq m
and
1\leq j\leq n
. So in this section, we will view
f
and
g
as functions from
[m]\times[n+1]
to
\mathbb{F}_{3}
and
[m+1]\times[n]
to
\mathbb{F}_{3}
respectively. Since the condition for a state to be admissible is a linear condition in this case, it is significantly easier to find the number of admissible states.

Theorem 7
.

The number of admissible states of the eight-vertex model is
2^{m+n+mn}
.

Proof.

Let
V
be the
\mathbb{F}_{2}
-vector space of pairs of functions
(f,g)
with
f:[m]\times[n+1]\to\mathbb{F}_{2}
and
g:[m+1]\times[n]\to\mathbb{F}_{2}
. Let
W
be
mn
-dimensional vector space with basis vectors
e_{i,j}
for
1\leq i\leq m
and
1\leq j\leq n
. Define a linear map
\varphi:V\to W
by

\varphi(f,g)=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{n}(f_{i,j}+g_{i,j}+f_{i,j+1}+g_{i+1,j})e_{i,j}.

By equation (
2
), we may view
V^{\prime}=\ker\varphi
as the set of admissible states of the eight-vertex model. Fix some
a\in[m]
and
b\in[n]
. Let
g=0
and define
f
by
f_{i,j}=0
if
i\neq a
or
j\leq b
, and
f_{i,j}=1
otherwise. Then,

\displaystyle\varphi(f,g)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{n}(f_{i,j}+f_{i,j+1})e_{i,j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=b}^{n}(f_{a,j}+f_{a,j+1})e_{a,j}

\displaystyle=e_{a,b}.

Since
a
and
b
were arbitrary, this shows that
\varphi
is surjective. Thus,

\displaystyle\dim V^{\prime}=\dim V-\dim W=m(n+1)+(m+1)n-mn=mn+m+n.

So
V^{\prime}
contains
2^{m+n+mn}
elements, and the result follows.
∎

Now we focus our attention on the number of admissible states with a given set of boundary conditions.

Theorem 8
.

There exists an admissible state
(f,g)
with boundary conditions

f_{i,1},f_{i,n+1},g_{1,j},g_{m+1,j}

if and only if

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{m}(f_{i,1}+f_{i,n+1})+\sum_{j=1}^{n}(g_{1,j}+g_{m+1,j})=0.

(3)

Proof.

Consider an admissible state
(f,g)
with vertical edges
f_{i,j}
and horizontal edges
g_{i,j}
. By equation (
2
), we have

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{n}(f_{i,j}+g_{i,j}+f_{i,j+1}+g_{i+1,j})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{n}(f_{i,j}+g_{i,j})+\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=2}^{n+1}f_{i,j}+\sum_{i=2}^{m+1}\sum_{j=1}^{n}g_{i,j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\left(f_{i,1}+\sum_{j=2}^{n}f_{i,j}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}g_{i,j}+f_{i,n+1}+\sum_{j=2}^{n}f_{i,j}\right)+\sum_{i=2}^{m+1}\sum_{j=1}^{n}g_{i,j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{m}(f_{i,1}+f_{i,n+1})+\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{n}g_{i,j}+\sum_{i=2}^{m+1}\sum_{j=1}^{n}g_{i,j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{m}(f_{i,1}+f_{i,n+1})+\sum_{j=1}^{n}(g_{1,j}+g_{m+1,j}).

To prove the converse, let

f_{i,1},f_{i,n+1},g_{1,j},g_{m+1,j}

be prescribed boundary conditions such that equation (
3
) holds. We will find an admissible state
(f,g)
with these boundary conditions. Let us define

g_{i,j}=0

for
2\leq i\leq m
and
2\leq j\leq n
, and

g_{i,1}=\sum_{b=1}^{n}g_{1,b}+\sum_{a=1}^{i-1}(f_{a,1}+f_{a,n+1})

for
2\leq i\leq m
. We also define

f_{i,j}=f_{i,n+1}

for
2\leq i\leq m-1
and
2\leq j\leq n
,

f_{1,j}=f_{1,n+1}+\sum_{b=1}^{n-j+1}g_{1,n-b+1}

for
2\leq j\leq n
, and

f_{m,j}=f_{m,n+1}+\sum_{b=1}^{n-j+1}g_{m+1,n-b+1}

for
2\leq j\leq n
. We claim that this defines an admissible state. To see this, we will look at several different cases. If
i=1
and
j=1
, then

\displaystyle f_{i,j}+f_{i,j+1}+g_{i,j}+g_{i+1,j}

\displaystyle=f_{1,1}+\left(f_{1,n+1}+\sum_{b=1}^{n-1}g_{1,n-b+1}\right)

\displaystyle+g_{1,1}+\left(\sum_{b=1}^{n}g_{1,b}+f_{1,1}+f_{1,n+1}\right)

\displaystyle=0.

If
i=1
and
2\leq j\leq n-1
, then

\displaystyle f_{i,j}+f_{i,j+1}+g_{i,j}+g_{i+1,j}

\displaystyle=\left(f_{1,n+1}+\sum_{b=1}^{n-j+1}g_{1,n-b+1}\right)+\left(f_{1,n+1}+\sum_{b=1}^{n-j}g_{1,n-b+1}\right)+g_{1,j}+0

\displaystyle=0.

If
i=1
and
j=n
, then

\displaystyle f_{i,j}+f_{i,j+1}+g_{i,j}+g_{i+1,j}

\displaystyle=\left(f_{1,n+1}+g_{1,n}\right)+f_{1,n+1}+g_{1,n}+0

\displaystyle=0.

If
2\leq i\leq m-1
and
j=1
, then

\displaystyle f_{i,j}+f_{i,j+1}+g_{i,j}+g_{i+1,j}

\displaystyle=f_{i,1}+f_{i,n+1}+\left(\sum_{b=1}^{n}g_{1,b}+\sum_{a=1}^{i-1}(f_{a,1}+f_{a,n+1})\right)

\displaystyle+\left(\sum_{b=1}^{n}g_{1,b}+\sum_{a=1}^{i}(f_{a,1}+f_{a,n+1})\right)

\displaystyle=0.

If
2\leq i\leq m-1
and
2\leq j\leq n-1
, then

\displaystyle f_{i,j}+f_{i,j+1}+g_{i,j}+g_{i+1,j}

\displaystyle=f_{i,n+1}+f_{i,n+1}+0+0

\displaystyle=0.

If
2\leq i\leq m-1
and
j=n
, then

\displaystyle f_{i,j}+f_{i,j+1}+g_{i,j}+g_{i+1,j}

\displaystyle=f_{i,n+1}+f_{i,n+1}+0+0

\displaystyle=0.

If
i=m
and
j=1
, then by equation (
3
),

\displaystyle f_{i,j}+f_{i,j+1}+g_{i,j}+g_{i+1,j}

\displaystyle=f_{m,1}+\left(f_{m,n+1}+\sum_{b=1}^{n-1}g_{m+1,n-b+1}\right)

\displaystyle+\left(\sum_{b=1}^{n}g_{1,b}+\sum_{a=1}^{m-1}(f_{a,1}+f_{a,n+1})\right)+g_{m+1,1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{b=1}^{n}(g_{1,b}+g_{m+1,b})+\sum_{a=1}^{m}(f_{a,1}+f_{a,n+1})

\displaystyle=0.

If
i=m
and
2\leq j\leq n-1
, then

\displaystyle f_{i,j}+f_{i,j+1}+g_{i,j}+g_{i+1,j}

\displaystyle=\left(f_{m,n+1}+\sum_{b=1}^{n-j+1}g_{m+1,n-b+1}\right)+\left(f_{m,n+1}+\sum_{b=1}^{n-j}g_{m+1,n-b+1}\right)

\displaystyle+0+g_{m+1,j}

\displaystyle=0.

If
i=m
and
j=n
, then

\displaystyle f_{i,j}+f_{i,j+1}+g_{i,j}+g_{i+1,j}

\displaystyle=\left(f_{m,n+1}+g_{m+1,n}\right)+f_{m,n+1}+0+g_{m+1,n}

\displaystyle=0.

Thus, the state we have defined is admissible.
∎

Theorem 9
.

Let
f_{i,1},f_{i,n+1},g_{1,j},g_{m+1,j}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}
be values such that

\sum_{i=1}^{m}(f_{i,1}+f_{i,n+1})+\sum_{j=1}^{n}(g_{1,j}+g_{m+1,j})=0.

Then the number of admissible states with boundary conditions
f_{i,1},f_{i,n+1},g_{1,j},g_{m+1,j}
is
2^{(m-1)(n-1)}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{S}
denote the set of admissible states with the boundary conditions above, and let
\mathcal{S}_{0}
denote the set of admissible states with boundary values all 0. We claim that
\mathcal{S}
and
\mathcal{S}_{0}
have the same number of elements. To see this, note first that
\mathcal{S}
is non-empty by Theorem
8
, so let
(f^{1},g^{1})\in\mathcal{S}
. Then we define a map
\psi:\mathcal{S}_{0}\to\mathcal{S}
by

\psi(f^{0},g^{0})=(f^{0}+f^{1},g^{0}+g^{1}).

It is easy to see that
\psi
is injective. To see that
\psi
is surjective, suppose that
(f^{2},g^{2})\in\mathcal{S}
. Then
(f^{2}-f^{1},g^{2}-g^{1})\in\mathcal{S}_{0}
, so
\psi(f^{2}-f^{1},g^{2}-g^{1})=(f^{2},g^{2})
. Thus,
\psi
is a bijection between
\mathcal{S}_{0}
and
\mathcal{S}
. This shows that all sets of boundary conditions having at least one admissible state have the same number of admissible states. It is not difficult to see that the number of sets of boundary conditions satisfying (
3
) is
2^{2m+2n-1}
. But by Theorem
7
, there are
2^{m+n+mn}
admissible states across all boundary conditions. So for a given set of boundary conditions, there are

\frac{2^{m+n+mn}}{2^{2m+2n-1}}=2^{(m-1)(n-1)}

admissible states.
∎

5
Yang-Baxter Equation for the Eight-Vertex Model

We continue to study the eight-vertex model. First, we review some basic properties of the Yang-Baxter equation. To each admissible labeling, we assign a value in some field
\mathbb{F}
, which we call its
Boltzmann weight
.

\hline\cr c_{1}&c_{-1}&d_{1}&d_{-1}\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

Figure 6:
Admissible labelings, along with their Boltzmann weights, for diagonally oriented vertices.

To each such assignment of Boltzmann weights, we associate a matrix

R=\begin{pmatrix}a_{1}&&&d_{1}\\
&b_{1}&c_{1}&\\
&c_{-1}&b_{-1}&\\
d_{-1}&&&a_{-1}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}a_{1}(R)&&&d_{1}(R)\\
&b_{1}(R)&c_{1}(R)&\\
&c_{-1}(R)&b_{-1}(R)&\\
d_{-1}(R)&&&a_{-1}(R)\end{pmatrix}.

Let
V
be a vector space with basis
v_{0}
and
v_{1}
. Then we may view
R
as an endomorphism of
V\otimes V
with respect to the basis
v_{0}\otimes v_{0}
,
v_{0}\otimes v_{1}
,
v_{1}\otimes v_{0}
, and
v_{1}\otimes v_{1}
. We write

R(v_{\nu}\otimes v_{\beta})=\sum_{\theta,\gamma}R_{\nu\beta}^{\theta\gamma}v_{\theta}\otimes v_{\gamma}.

For example, we have
R_{01}^{10}=c_{-1}(R)
. Observe that
R_{\nu\beta}^{\theta\gamma}
is the Boltzmann weight of the following labelings.

\nu
\beta
\theta
\gamma
\bullet

\nu
\beta
\theta
\gamma
\bullet

Now if
\phi
is an endormorphism of
V\otimes V
, we define endomorphisms
\phi_{12},\phi_{23},\phi_{13}
of
V\otimes V\otimes V
as follows. If
\phi=\phi^{\prime}\otimes\phi^{\prime\prime}
for
\phi^{\prime},\phi^{\prime\prime}\in\text{End}(V)
, then we define

\displaystyle\phi_{12}

\displaystyle=\phi^{\prime}\otimes\phi^{\prime\prime}\otimes 1

\displaystyle\phi_{13}

\displaystyle=\phi^{\prime}\otimes 1\otimes\phi^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle\phi_{23}

\displaystyle=1\otimes\phi\otimes\phi^{\prime\prime}.

We extend these definitions for all
\phi
by linearity. For
\phi,\psi,\chi\in\text{End}(V\otimes V\otimes V)
, we define their
Yang-Baxter commutator

[[\phi,\psi,\chi]]
by

[[\phi,\psi,\chi]]=\phi_{12}\psi_{13}\chi_{23}-\chi_{23}\psi_{13}\phi_{12}.

For
R,S,T\in\text{End}(V\otimes V)
, we say that the
star-triangle relation
holds if

\hfill\sum_{\gamma,\mu,\nu}\quad\hbox to131.28pt{\vbox to131.28pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 8.7359pt\lower-8.7359pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}
{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}}{{{}}{{}}}{}{{}}{}{}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 39.37 C 43.6 39.37 35.14 118.11 78.74 118.11 L 157.48 118.11}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}
{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}}{{{}}{{}}}{}{{}}{}{}{}

(4)

Here we associate a lattice state with the product of the Boltzmann weights of its vertices. In other words, the star-triangle relation holds when

\sum_{\gamma,\mu,\nu}R_{\sigma\tau}^{\nu\mu}S_{\nu\beta}^{\theta\gamma}T_{\mu\gamma}^{\rho\alpha}=\sum_{\delta,\phi,\psi}T_{\tau\beta}^{\psi\delta}S_{\sigma\delta}^{\phi\alpha}R_{\phi\psi}^{\theta\rho}.

The main fact we will use in order to do computations is that the star-triangle relation is equivalent to the vanishing of the Yang-Baxter commutator. For a proof of this statement, see
[
2
]
.

Lemma 10
.

Let
R,S,T\in\operatorname{End}(V\otimes V)
. Then the star-triangle relation holds for
R,S,T
if and only if
[[R,S,T]]=0
.

Given matrices
S
and
T
, we will determine necessary conditions for there to exist
R
such that
[[R,S,T]]=0
. We will follow the approach of Galleas and Martins
[
7
]
. Our analysis will be more general, since we do not assume that the Boltzmann weights
c_{1},c_{-1},d_{1},d_{-1}
satisfy
c_{1}=c_{-1}
and
d_{1}=d_{-1}
. Suppose that
R,S,T\in\text{End}(V\otimes V)
are Boltzmann weights such that
[[R,S,T]]=0
. Moreover, assume that
a_{1}(S)
,
b_{1}(S)
,
c_{1}(S)
,
d_{1}(S)
,
a_{2}(S)
,
b_{2}(S)
,
c_{2}(S)
,
d_{2}(S)
,
a_{1}(T)
,
b_{1}(T)
,
c_{1}(T)
,
d_{1}(T)
,
a_{2}(T)
,
b_{2}(T)
,
c_{2}(T)
,
d_{2}(T)
are nonzero. A computation shows that the condition
[[R,S,T]]=0
can be expressed as the system of 28 equations

\displaystyle a_{j}(T)a_{j}(S)d_{i}(R)+d_{i}(T)c_{i}(S)a_{-j}(R)

\displaystyle=c_{i}(T)d_{i}(S)a_{j}(R)+b_{-j}(T)b_{-j}(S)d_{i}(R)

(5)

\displaystyle d_{i}(T)b_{j}(S)c_{i}(R)+a_{j}(T)d_{i}(S)b_{-j}(R)

\displaystyle=b_{j}(T)d_{i}(S)a_{j}(R)+c_{-i}(T)b_{-j}(S)d_{i}(R)

(6)

\displaystyle d_{i}(T)b_{j}(S)b_{j}(R)+a_{j}(T)d_{i}(S)c_{-i}(R)

\displaystyle=d_{i}(T)a_{j}(S)a_{j}(R)+a_{-j}(T)c_{-i}(S)d_{i}(R)

(7)

\displaystyle c_{i}(T)a_{j}(S)c_{i}(R)+b_{j}(T)c_{i}(S)b_{-j}(R)

\displaystyle=a_{j}(T)c_{i}(S)a_{j}(R)+d_{-i}(T)a_{-j}(S)d_{i}(R)

(8)

\displaystyle c_{i}(T)a_{j}(S)b_{j}(R)+b_{j}(T)c_{i}(S)c_{-i}(R)

\displaystyle=c_{i}(T)b_{j}(S)a_{j}(R)+b_{-j}(T)d_{-i}(S)d_{i}(R)

(9)

\displaystyle b_{-j}(T)a_{j}(S)c_{i}(R)+c_{-i}(T)c_{i}(S)b_{-j}(R)

\displaystyle=d_{-i}(T)d_{i}(S)b_{j}(R)+a_{j}(T)b_{-j}(S)c_{i}(R)

(10)

\displaystyle c_{1}(T)c_{-1}(S)c_{1}(R)

\displaystyle=c_{-1}(T)c_{1}(S)c_{-1}(R)

(11)

\displaystyle d_{1}(T)c_{1}(S)d_{-1}(R)

\displaystyle=d_{-1}(T)c_{-1}(S)d_{1}(R)

(12)

\displaystyle c_{1}(T)d_{1}(S)d_{-1}(R)

\displaystyle=c_{-1}(T)d_{-1}(S)d_{1}(R)

(13)

\displaystyle d_{1}(T)d_{-1}(S)c_{1}(R)

\displaystyle=d_{-1}(T)d_{1}(S)c_{-1}(R)

(14)

for
i,j\in\{-1,1\}
. By equation
(k,r,s)
, we mean equation
k
with
i=r
and
j=s
substituted in. Solving for
a_{-j}(R)
in (
8
,
i,-j
) gives us

\displaystyle a_{-j}(R)=\frac{c_{i}(T)a_{-j}(S)c_{i}(R)+b_{-j}(T)c_{i}(S)b_{j}(R)-d_{-i}(T)a_{j}(S)d_{i}(R)}{a_{-j}(T)c_{i}(S)}

(15)

and solving for
a_{j}(R)
in (
9
,
i,j
) gives us

\displaystyle a_{j}(R)=\frac{c_{i}(T)a_{j}(S)b_{j}(R)+b_{j}(T)c_{i}(S)c_{-i}(R)-b_{-j}(T)d_{-i}(S)d_{i}(R)}{c_{i}(T)b_{j}(S)}.

(16)

Substituting (
15
,
i,j
) and (
16
,
i,j
) into (
5
,
i,j
) yields

\displaystyle a_{j}(T)a_{j}(S)d_{i}(R)

\displaystyle+d_{i}(T)c_{i}(S)\left(\frac{c_{i}(T)a_{-j}(S)c_{i}(R)+b_{-j}(T)c_{i}(S)b_{j}(R)-d_{-i}(T)a_{j}(S)d_{i}(R)}{a_{-j}(T)c_{i}(S)}\right)

\displaystyle=c_{i}(T)d_{i}(S)\left(\frac{c_{i}(T)a_{j}(S)b_{j}(R)+b_{j}(T)c_{i}(S)c_{-i}(R)-b_{-j}(T)d_{-i}(S)d_{i}(R)}{c_{i}(T)b_{j}(S)}\right)

\displaystyle+b_{-j}(T)b_{-j}(S)d_{i}(R).

Rearranging this equation gives us

\displaystyle b_{j}(R)[d_{i}(T)b_{-j}(T)c_{i}(S)b_{j}(S)-d_{i}(S)c_{i}(T)a_{j}(S)a_{-j}(T)]=-c_{i}(R)[d_{i}(T)b_{j}(S)c_{i}(T)a_{-j}(S)]

\displaystyle+c_{-i}(R)[d_{i}(S)b_{j}(T)c_{i}(S)a_{-j}(T)]+d_{i}(R)[a_{-j}(T)b_{-j}(T)[b_{j}(S)b_{-j}(S)-d_{i}(S)d_{-i}(S)]

\displaystyle+a_{j}(S)b_{j}(S)[d_{i}(T)d_{-i}(T)-a_{j}(T)a_{-j}(T)]].

(17)

Equation (
11
,
i,j
) implies that

\displaystyle c_{-i}(R)

\displaystyle=c_{i}(R)\frac{c_{i}(T)c_{-i}(S)}{c_{-i}(T)c_{i}(S)}.

(18)

Substituting this expression for
c_{-i}(R)
into (
17
,
i,j
) gives us

\displaystyle b_{j}(R)[d_{i}(T)b_{-j}(T)c_{i}(S)b_{j}(S)-d_{i}(S)c_{i}(T)a_{j}(S)a_{-j}(T)]

\displaystyle=c_{i}(R)\left[\frac{-c_{-i}(T)d_{i}(T)b_{j}(S)c_{i}(T)a_{-j}(S)+c_{i}(T)c_{-i}(S)d_{i}(S)b_{j}(T)a_{-j}(T)}{c_{-i}(T)}\right]

\displaystyle+d_{i}(R)[a_{-j}(T)b_{-j}(T)[b_{j}(S)b_{-j}(S)-d_{i}(S)d_{-i}(S)]

\displaystyle+a_{j}(S)b_{j}(S)[d_{i}(T)d_{-i}(T)-a_{j}(T)a_{-j}(T)]]

(19)

We repeat the above process for equations
6
and
7
. Solving for
a_{-j}(R)
in (
6
,
i,-j
) gives us

\displaystyle a_{-j}(R)

\displaystyle=\frac{d_{i}(T)b_{-j}(S)c_{i}(R)+a_{-j}(T)d_{i}(S)b_{j}(R)-c_{-i}(T)b_{j}(S)d_{i}(R)}{b_{-j}(T)d_{i}(S)}

(20)

and solving for
a_{j}(R)
in (
7
,
i,j
) gives us

\displaystyle a_{j}(R)

\displaystyle=\frac{d_{i}(T)b_{j}(S)b_{j}(R)+a_{j}(T)d_{i}(S)c_{-i}(R)-a_{-j}(T)c_{-i}(S)d_{i}(R)}{d_{i}(T)a_{j}(S)}.

(21)

Substituting (
20
,
i,j
) and (
21
,
i,j
) into (
5
,
i,-j
) yields

\displaystyle a_{-j}(T)a_{-j}(S)d_{i}(R)

\displaystyle+d_{i}(T)c_{i}(S)\left(\frac{d_{i}(T)b_{j}(S)b_{j}(R)+a_{j}(T)d_{i}(S)c_{-i}(R)-a_{-j}(T)c_{-i}(S)d_{i}(R)}{d_{i}(T)a_{j}(S)}\right)

\displaystyle=c_{i}(T)d_{i}(S)\left(\frac{d_{i}(T)b_{-j}(S)c_{i}(R)+a_{-j}(T)d_{i}(S)b_{j}(R)-c_{-i}(T)b_{j}(S)d_{i}(R)}{b_{-j}(T)d_{i}(S)}\right)

\displaystyle+b_{j}(T)b_{j}(S)d_{i}(R).

Rearranging this equation gives us

\displaystyle b_{j}(R)[c_{i}(S)d_{i}(T)b_{j}(S)b_{-j}(T)-c_{i}(T)a_{-j}(T)d_{i}(S)a_{j}(S)]=c_{i}(R)[c_{i}(T)d_{i}(T)b_{-j}(S)a_{j}(S)]

\displaystyle-c_{-i}(R)[c_{i}(S)a_{j}(T)d_{i}(S)b_{-j}(T)]+d_{i}(R)[a_{-j}(T)b_{-j}(T)[c_{i}(S)c_{-i}(S)-a_{j}(S)a_{-j}(S)]

\displaystyle+a_{j}(S)b_{j}(S)[b_{j}(T)b_{-j}(T)-c_{i}(T)c_{-i}(T)]].

(22)

Substituting (
18
,
i,j
) into (
22
,
i,j
) yields

\displaystyle b_{j}(R)[c_{i}(S)d_{i}(T)b_{j}(S)b_{-j}(T)-c_{i}(T)a_{-j}(T)d_{i}(S)a_{j}(S)]

\displaystyle=c_{i}(R)\left[\frac{c_{-i}(T)c_{i}(T)d_{i}(T)b_{-j}(S)a_{j}(S)-c_{i}(T)c_{-i}(S)a_{j}(T)d_{i}(S)b_{-j}(T)}{c_{-i}(T)}\right]

\displaystyle+d_{i}(R)[a_{-j}(T)b_{-j}(T)[c_{i}(S)c_{-i}(S)-a_{j}(S)a_{-j}(S)]

\displaystyle+a_{j}(S)b_{j}(S)[b_{j}(T)b_{-j}(T)-c_{i}(T)c_{-i}(T)]].

(23)

Since the left hand sides of (
19
,
i,j
) and (
23
,
i,j
) are identical, setting their right hand sides equal to each other gives us

\displaystyle\frac{c_{i}(R)c_{i}(T)}{c_{-i}(T)}[c_{-i}(T)d_{i}(T)[b_{-j}(S)a_{j}(S)+b_{j}(S)a_{-j}(S)]

\displaystyle-c_{-i}(S)d_{i}(S)[a_{j}(T)b_{-j}(T)+b_{j}(T)a_{-j}(T)]]

\displaystyle=d_{i}(R)[a_{-j}(T)b_{-j}(T)F(S)-a_{j}(S)b_{j}(S)F(T)]

(24)

where

F(\psi)=a_{1}(\psi)a_{-1}(\psi)+b_{1}(\psi)b_{-1}(\psi)-c_{1}(\psi)c_{-1}(\psi)-d_{1}(\psi)d_{-1}(\psi).

Now we repeat everything above, except with equation (
10
) in place of equation (
5
), and the variables
b
instead of
a
. We can rewrite equation (
6
,
i,j
) as

\displaystyle d_{i}(S)b_{-j}(R)

\displaystyle=\frac{b_{j}(T)d_{i}(S)a_{j}(R)+c_{-i}(T)b_{-j}(S)d_{i}(R)-d_{i}(T)b_{j}(S)c_{i}(R)}{a_{j}(T)}

(25)

and equation (
9
,
-i,j
) as

\displaystyle c_{-i}(T)b_{j}(R)=\frac{c_{-i}(T)b_{j}(S)a_{j}(R)+b_{-j}(T)d_{i}(S)d_{-i}(R)-b_{j}(T)c_{-i}(S)c_{i}(R)}{a_{j}(S)}.

(26)

Substituting equations (
25
,
i,j
) and (
26
,
i,j
) into (
10
,
i,-j
) yields

\displaystyle a_{j}(R)[c_{i}(S)c_{-i}(T)b_{j}(S)a_{j}(T)-d_{-i}(T)b_{j}(T)d_{i}(S)a_{j}(S)]

\displaystyle=c_{i}(R)[a_{j}(T)b_{j}(T)[c_{i}(S)c_{-i}(S)-a_{-j}(S)a_{j}(S)]+a_{j}(S)b_{j}(S)[a_{-j}(T)a_{j}(T)-d_{i}(T)d_{-i}(T)]]

\displaystyle+d_{i}(R)[d_{-i}(T)c_{-i}(T)b_{-j}(S)a_{j}(S)]-d_{-i}(R)[c_{i}(S)b_{-j}(T)d_{i}(S)a_{j}(T)].

(27)

Equation (
12
,
i
) implies that

\displaystyle d_{-i}(R)=d_{i}(R)\frac{d_{-i}(T)c_{-i}(S)}{d_{i}(T)c_{i}(S)}.

(28)

Substituting this expression into (
27
,
i,j
) gives us

\displaystyle a_{j}(R)[c_{i}(S)c_{-i}(T)b_{j}(S)a_{j}(T)-d_{-i}(T)b_{j}(T)d_{i}(S)a_{j}(S)]

\displaystyle=c_{i}(R)[a_{j}(T)b_{j}(T)[c_{i}(S)c_{-i}(S)-a_{-j}(S)a_{j}(S)]+a_{j}(S)b_{j}(S)[a_{-j}(T)a_{j}(T)-d_{i}(T)d_{-i}(T)]]

\displaystyle+\frac{d_{i}(R)}{d_{i}(T)}[d_{i}(T)d_{-i}(T)c_{-i}(T)b_{-j}(S)a_{j}(S)-d_{-i}(T)c_{-i}(S)b_{-j}(T)d_{i}(S)a_{j}(T)].

(29)

We can rewrite equation (
7
,
-i,j
) as

\displaystyle d_{-i}(T)b_{j}(R)=\frac{d_{-i}(T)a_{j}(S)a_{j}(R)+a_{-j}(T)c_{i}(S)d_{-i}(R)-a_{j}(T)d_{-i}(S)c_{i}(R)}{b_{j}(S)}

(30)

and equation (
8
,
i,j
) as

\displaystyle c_{i}(S)b_{-j}(R)=\frac{a_{j}(T)c_{i}(S)a_{j}(R)+d_{-i}(T)a_{-j}(S)d_{i}(R)-c_{i}(T)a_{j}(S)c_{i}(R)}{b_{j}(T)}.

(31)

Substituting equations (
30
,
i,j
) and (
31
,
i,j
) into (
10
,
i,j
) gives us

\displaystyle a_{j}(R)[c_{-i}(T)a_{j}(T)c_{i}(S)b_{j}(S)-d_{i}(S)d_{-i}(T)a_{j}(S)b_{j}(T)]

\displaystyle=c_{i}(R)[a_{j}(S)b_{j}(S)[c_{i}(T)c_{-i}(T)-b_{j}(T)b_{-j}(T)]+a_{j}(T)b_{j}(T)[b_{j}(S)b_{-j}(S)-d_{i}(S)d_{-i}(S)]]

\displaystyle+d_{-i}(R)[d_{i}(S)a_{-j}(T)c_{i}(S)b_{j}(T)]-d_{i}(R)[c_{-i}(T)d_{-i}(T)a_{-j}(S)b_{j}(S)].

(32)

Substituting equation (
28
,
i,j
) into (
32
,
i,j
) gives us

\displaystyle a_{j}(R)[c_{-i}(T)a_{j}(T)c_{i}(S)b_{j}(S)-d_{i}(S)d_{-i}(T)a_{j}(S)b_{j}(T)]

\displaystyle=c_{i}(R)[a_{j}(S)b_{j}(S)[c_{i}(T)c_{-i}(T)-b_{j}(T)b_{-j}(T)]+a_{j}(T)b_{j}(T)[b_{j}(S)b_{-j}(S)-d_{i}(S)d_{-i}(S)]]

\displaystyle+\frac{d_{i}(R)}{d_{i}(T)}[d_{-i}(T)c_{-i}(S)d_{i}(S)a_{-j}(T)b_{j}(T)-d_{i}(T)c_{-i}(T)d_{-i}(T)a_{-j}(S)b_{j}(S)].

(33)

Since equations (
29
,
i,j
) and (
33
,
i,j
) have the same left hand side, we have

\displaystyle c_{i}(R)[a_{j}(T)b_{j}(T)F(S)-a_{j}(S)b_{j}(S)F(T)]

\displaystyle=\frac{d_{i}(R)d_{-i}(T)}{d_{i}(T)}[c_{-i}(T)d_{i}(T)[b_{-j}(S)a_{j}(S)+a_{-j}(S)b_{j}(S)]-c_{-i}(S)d_{i}(S)[b_{-j}(T)a_{j}(T)+a_{-j}(T)b_{j}(T)]].

(34)

Let

\displaystyle G_{i}(S,T)=[c_{-i}(T)d_{i}(T)[b_{-1}(S)a_{1}(S)+a_{-1}(S)b_{1}(S)]-c_{-i}(S)d_{i}(S)[b_{-1}(T)a_{1}(T)+a_{-1}(T)b_{1}(T)]].

Our calculations above allow us to prove the following condition.

Theorem 11
.

Let
S,T\in\operatorname{End}(V\otimes V)
. Suppose that there exists
R\in\text{End}(V\otimes V)
such that
c_{-1}(R),c_{1}(R),d_{-1}(R),d_{1}(R)
are nonzero and
[[R,S,T]]=0
. Then, we have

\displaystyle a_{1}(T)b_{1}(T)F(S)

\displaystyle=a_{-1}(T)b_{-1}(T)F(S)

(35)

\displaystyle a_{1}(S)b_{1}(S)F(T)

\displaystyle=a_{-1}(S)b_{-1}(S)F(T)

(36)

\displaystyle\frac{c_{i}(T)d_{-i}(T)}{c_{-i}(T)d_{i}(T)}G_{i}(S,T)^{2}

\displaystyle=[a_{1}(T)b_{1}(T)F(S)-a_{1}(S)b_{1}(S)F(T)]^{2}

(37)

\displaystyle\frac{c_{1}(T)c_{-1}(S)}{c_{-1}(T)c_{1}(S)}

\displaystyle=\frac{d_{1}(T)d_{-1}(S)}{d_{-1}(T)d_{1}(S)}.

(38)

Proof.

We write the system of equations given by
(\ref{eqn:20})
and
(\ref{eqn:30})
as the matrix equation

\begin{pmatrix}\frac{c_{i}(T)}{c_{-i}(T)}G_{i}(S,T)&&-\alpha_{1}(S,T)\\
\frac{c_{i}(T)}{c_{-i}(T)}G_{i}(S,T)&&-\alpha_{2}(S,T)\\
\beta_{1}(S,T)&&-\frac{d_{-i}(T)}{d_{i}(T)}G_{i}(S,T)\\
\beta_{2}(S,T)&&-\frac{d_{-i}(T)}{d_{i}(T)}G_{i}(S,T)\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}c_{i}(R)\\
d_{i}(R)\end{pmatrix}=0,

where

\alpha_{j}(S,T)=a_{j}(T)b_{j}(T)F(S)-a_{-j}(S)b_{-j}(S)F(T)

and

\beta_{j}(S,T)=a_{j}(T)b_{j}(T)F(S)-a_{j}(S)b_{j}(S)F(T).

First we observe that if
G_{i}(S,T)=0
, then we must have
\alpha_{1}(S,T)=\alpha_{2}(S,T)=\beta_{1}(S,T)=\beta_{2}(S,T)=0
in order for both
c_{i}(R)
and
d_{i}(R)
to be nonzero. It is easy to see that this implies equations (
35
), (
36
), (
37
). Now suppose that
G_{i}(S,T)\neq 0
. A necessary condition for one of
c_{-1}(R),c_{1}(R),d_{-1}(R),
and
d_{1}(R)
to be nonzero is for all 6 of the 2 by 2 minors of the above matrix to vanish for
i=1,-1
. The vanishing of the minors of the rows
(1,2),(3,4),(1,3)
can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}(S,T)

\displaystyle=\alpha_{2}(S,T)

(39)

\displaystyle\beta_{1}(S,T)

\displaystyle=\beta_{2}(S,T)

(40)

\displaystyle\frac{c_{i}(T)d_{-i}(T)}{c_{-i}(T)d_{i}(T)}G_{i}(S,T)^{2}

\displaystyle=\alpha_{1}(S,T)\beta_{1}(S,T).

(41)

By combining equations (
39
) and (
40
), we obtain conditions (
35
) and (
36
). Then, using equations (
41
) and (
35
),we get

\displaystyle\frac{c_{i}(T)d_{-i}(T)}{c_{-i}(T)d_{i}(T)}G_{i}(S,T)^{2}

\displaystyle=[a_{1}(T)b_{1}(T)F(S)-a_{2}(S)b_{2}(S)F(T)][a_{1}(T)b_{1}(T)F(S)-a_{1}(S)b_{1}(S)F(T)]

\displaystyle=[a_{1}(T)b_{1}(T)F(S)-a_{1}(S)b_{1}(S)F(T)][a_{1}(T)b_{1}(T)F(S)-a_{1}(S)b_{1}(S)F(T)],

which shows that (
37
) holds. So in all cases, conditions (
35
), (
36
), and (
37
) hold.

To get the last equation, we combine equations (
11
,
i
) and (
14
,
i
).
∎

6
Future Directions

A main theme of this paper was interpreting properties of lattice models in terms of differential forms. We have only scratched the surface of the problems this technique can be applied to. In particular, we restricted our attention to lattice models without weights assigned at each vertex. A first step toward understanding weighted partition functions in this viewpoint would be to find an interpretation of the Yang-Baxter equation using differential forms.

Another potential area of further investigation is to describe interesting families of weights for which the eight-vertex Yang-Baxter equation holds. This has been explored in the case
c_{1}=c_{-1}
and
d_{1}=d_{-1}
by Cuerno et al
[
5
]
. It is natural to ask if the weights can be generalized to families where
c_{1}\neq c_{-1}
or
d_{1}\neq d_{-1}
.

7
